Met Gala's New Theme Is the Biggest Recession Indicator Yet

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Brace yourself for A-listers strutting down the red carpet in everyday garb like jeans, T-shirts, and "humble, even 'disposable' materials."

Two months after British fashion designer John Galliano bowed out of his 2027 Met Gala and Costume Institute exhibition due to backlash over his racist past, organizers have decided to play it safe with the new theme announced today, November 5.

In perhaps the biggest recession indicator yet, extraOrdinary: Fashion and the Poetics of the Everyday will see A-listers and Hollywood's finest strutting down the red carpet in everyday garb like jeans, T-shirts, and "humble, even 'disposable' materials."

When The Met announced the Costume Institute exhibition John Galliano: Horizons in July, intense scrutiny resurfaced around the designer, who was convicted of antisemitic and anti-Asian hate speech directed at a French couple in February 2011, and fined for a torrent of antisemitic slurs against a woman the same year. A separate video of Galliano declaring “I love Hitler” caused Dior to fire the designer from his post as creative director in March 2011. Amid the renewed backlash, Galliano withdrew his exhibition on August 31.

Omar Hamdi says AI will test whether PR firms can really scale

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Pathos chief Omar Hamdi argues that AI should make PR more efficient, measurable and useful to growth companies, rather than replace human judgement.

Omar Hamdi, founder and CEO of Pathos Communications, right, on a panel at Press Gazette’s Media Strategy Network USA in New York in March 2026.

City A.M. reported on 6 October that the share of UK firms spending on public relations and management consultants fell to 4.2% in September, even as overall business spending rose 3.1% year on year and spending on AI services increased rapidly.

For Omar Hamdi, chief executive of London-listed Pathos Communications plc, those figures are less a death notice for PR than a sharper test of its economics.

The question suggested by those figures is not only whether companies will cut agency budgets as AI tools spread. It is whether agencies can use technology to deliver more useful work, more consistently, at a price and margin that make sense.

Hamdi’s answer, by his account, is to build a PR company where software takes on more of the preparation, targeting and business development, while people still handle the relationships and judgement that matter when a company is trying to be understood.

Pathos’s recent numbers give that argument a public company frame. Investegate published Pathos’s results for the six months ended 30 June 2026 on 9 September, reporting 14% revenue growth, 36% adjusted EBITDA growth, an 80% rise in customer cash receipts and reoccurring customers accounting for 36% of revenue, compared with 16% a year earlier.

The same announcement said Pressella, its AI virtual publicist, and PathosMind remained on track for general availability in the first half of 2027.

Proactive Investors reported on the same day that Pathos’s first-half adjusted EBITDA rose as higher revenue, improved operating leverage and lower bad debt charges fed through to profitability. That is the operational point Hamdi keeps returning to. He says the firm’s technology is helping with lead generation, lead nurturing and onboarding, while repeat and reoccurring customer relationships are becoming a larger part of the business.

The practical change, as Hamdi describes it, starts before a prospect has spoken to anyone at Pathos. The technology analyses news, shares potential hooks and article ideas, and prepares materials for the human conversation that follows. By the time a meeting takes place, he says the team can have article drafts, business cases and current news hooks ready.

In delivery, he says Pathos is using algorithmic matching between client stories and publishers to improve the chances that content succeeds.

That matters because the addressable market Hamdi is thinking about is not a small pool of blue-chip communications departments. He points to a global market of 400 million SMEs.

In his telling, the technology is intended to make a serious PR experience available to companies that may not have had the time, budget or confidence to hire a conventional agency.

Prospects sometimes respond to outreach, he says, as if Pathos is already doing part of the publicist’s job. One reaction he recalls is: “I feel like you’re already my PR firm.”

The aim is not simply more automated content. Hamdi’s emphasis is on preparation that changes the quality of the first human interaction. He says clients felt they were getting the same experience they would receive if a highly qualified PR person with decades of experience had spent a day working with them, and were getting that experience “before they’d even met with us for the first time.”

There is also an internal productivity argument. Hamdi says the technology affects how the team works, not only how prospects are contacted.

“It just makes their job much more efficient, much more lubricated, the velocity of the business has improved,” he says. The efficiency of Pathos’s business development, he adds, is higher than it has ever been.

For business readers, the more interesting claim is not that AI can write faster. It is that what was originally a services company may be able to grow by becoming a technology company with a service layer. Hamdi says Pathos is adding larger and stronger publishers to its platform, which can support better margins with those publishers. “We’re getting more efficient as we scale,” he says.

Yahoo Finance reported on 15 October that Hamdi discussed rising repeat and reoccurring revenue, Pathos’s aim of building a sustainable, profitable listed business, and the planned first half 2027 launch of its AI-powered virtual publicist.

The broader bet is still to be tested as Pressella and PathosMind move toward wider availability. But the direction is clear enough. In Hamdi’s view, PR’s future belongs less to firms selling hours and more to operators that can use technology to scale judgement, preparation and relevance, without stripping out the human work that gives PR its value.

Appalachian Trail Threatened by ‘Uncommonly’ Large Wildfire in White Mountains

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The Wild River Fire burns in the White Mountain National Forest (Photo: Courtesy USFS)

A wildfire that may be the largest in White Mountains history is currently burning just miles from the Appalachian Trail, and officials say a human may have caused it.

The Wild River Fire started on Tuesday, November 3, in Coos County, New Hampshire, and has grown to 865 acres as of this writing, with just 11 percent contained. While the Appalachian Trail (AT), roughly 10 miles from the blaze, currently remains open, the U.S. Forest Service (USFS) has closed numerous other trails and roads in the area.

Small brush fires and wildfires occur in White Mountains National Forest (WMNF) almost every year, but according to Gorham Fire Chief Philip Cloutier, who spoke with NHPR on Wednesday, a wildfire of this size is unusual. Both extended drought and extremely dry fall conditions have fuelled its speedy growth.

“That’s very uncommon in our area,” Cloutier said. “The largest wildfire that we can remember was just under 100 acres, and that was over 20 years ago.”

Officially, USFS is still investigating the wildfire’s source. Authorities have indicated that they believe it was human-caused, though they have released no further details about the origin.

While the Wild River Fire is burning roughly 10 miles east of the AT, no closures or evacuations have been issued for the trail yet. Still, other trails in the area (some of which connect with the AT) have been closed. Below is the USFS list of trail closures in WMNF.

We will update this article if more closures are announced.

In addition to these closures, Wild River Road is also closed in its entirety.

According to the USFS, the response to the Wild River Fire has been swift, and additional resources, including thje Cherokee Hotshot Crew and a smoke suppression module, have been sent to aid in the fight. The area’s steep, rocky terrain, remote location, and wet conditions have made it particularly challenging for firefighters. A USFS spokesperson told NHPR that firefighters had to cross the Wild River on foot to mount a direct attack.

However, the efforts seem to be paying off. On November 4, the fire swelled to 982 acres. That evening, it was reduced to 865 acres, where it remains currently.

“I am proud of the firefighters who responded quickly and continue to work tirelessly to contain the Wild River Fire,” noted Josh Sjostrom, District Ranger for the White Mountain National Forest’s Androscoggin Ranger District. “We will continue to work with our neighbors and local and state partners to contain this fire.”

Kia’s EV5 spotted with an updated design and a huge new infotainment display

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Kia’s Sportage-sized electric SUV, the EV5, was spotted with a distinct new style and an overhauled interior, including a huge widescreen infotainment screen.

The EV5 is the fully electric SUV we want, but can’t have in the US. After launching it in China in 2023, Kia introduced the global version last year, which is now on sale in Europe, Canada, the UK, and other markets.

It has already been a big part of Kia’s comeback in China and is expected to become one of its best-selling EVs.

Kia appears to be preparing an updated version. A new EV5 model was recently spotted by ShortsCar in a South Korean parking garage without camouflage, and it’s expected to be the updated version.

You can see in the video that the design has changed quite a bit. Up front, the hood and headlights remain about the same, but new design elements like a black accent light are added.

It also features a unique bumper design, similar to that of the rugged EV5 WKNDR Concept Kia revealed last November at the Guangzhou Motor Show.

The biggest changes are inside, where the interior appears to be completely overhauled. Instead of the current large display screen spanning from the driver to the center, the new EV5 features a display that extends from the center to the passenger side.

There also appears to be an added slim display in front of the steering wheel. Since Hyundai has recently added a slim display to vehicles equipped with its Pleos infotainment system, the updated Kia EV5 is expected to feature the new setup.

However, this is still speculation and has not been confirmed. The center console has also changed. Instead of the physical buttons, it now has a cover, giving it a cleaner overall look.

According to a sticker on the back, the updated EV5 is set to launch in the Philippines. Given Kia initially launched the EV5 in China in 2023, it will be due for a facelift around 2027.

Kia has yet to confirm whether and when it will launch the updated EV5 in other global markets, such as Europe or Korea. It will likely arrive after the China-made version, like the current EV5. We should learn more details soon.

Blue Origin announces 3rd stage for its powerful New Glenn rocket —and a new engine

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Blue Origin's big rocket is getting a little bigger.

Jeff Bezos' company announced today (Nov. 5) that it has developed a third stage for its New Glenn rocket, which it says will make the powerful launcher more capable and flexible.

The stage, called the GS3, "enhances New Glenn's capabilities," Blue Origin said in a blog post today. "The third stage enables direct deployment of satellite constellations to GEO [geostationary orbit] and supports heavy-lift science and exploration missions to the moon and deep space."

New Glenn is a two-stage vehicle at the moment. Its reusable first stage is powered by seven of Blue Origin's BE-4 engines, and its second stage features two of the company's BE-3Us. The rocket stands 322 feet (98 meters) tall and can haul 14.3 tons (13 metric tons) to geostationary transfer orbit (GTO) and 50 tons (45 metric tons) to low Earth orbit (LEO).

These are the specs for the standard, or "7x2," version of New Glenn. Last year, Blue Origin announced that it's developing a super-heavy variant known as the 9x4, because it will feature nine first-stage BE-4s and four upper-stage BE-3Us. The 9x4 will be nearly 400 feet (122 m) tall, and it will be able to loft 15.4 tons (14 metric tons) to GTO and 77 tons (70 metric tons) to LEO, according to Blue Origin.

New Glenn has flown three times to date, each time in the 7x2 configuration. The rocket debuted in January 2025, then launched again in November 2025 and again on April 19 of this year.

Things didn't go according to plan on that latest mission. The rocket deployed AST SpaceMobile's BlueBird 7 communications satellite into the wrong orbit, and the spacecraft ended up being lost.

Another setback came just a month later: On May 28, New Glenn exploded on the pad during a routine engine test at Florida's Cape Canaveral Space Force Station. Blue Origin has been working ever since to rebuild the pad and get New Glenn ready for its fourth flight, which is expected to occur late this year.

We now know that Blue Origin has also been working on the GS3 while those repairs were underway. The new stage will be powered by one BE-7 engine, which the company developed for use on its Blue Moon spacecraft, one of the two private vehicles that NASA selected to be crewed lunar landers for its Artemis program. (The other is SpaceX's Starship.)

Blue Moon landing missions won't need to launch on GS3-equipped New Glenn rockets, according to Blue Origin. But the third stage "can support sustained operations around the moon," the company wrote in today's blog post. "By delivering significant payload mass directly to trans-lunar injection (TLI), GS3 enables faster transit times while preserving propellant for on-orbit operations."

And there's already demand for such services: Blue Origin also announced today that a government customer (which it did not name) has booked a GS3 mission to cislunar (Earth-moon) space, with launch targeted for 2028.

And the company dropped yet more news today — that it's working on a new first-stage engine, called Northstar.

Like Blue Origin's other booster engine, the BE-4, Northstar burns liquid oxygen and liquid methane propellant, a combination known as methalox. But the new engine is more powerful and less complex.

"Compared to the BE-4 engine, Northstar reduces mass by 30% and parts count by 50%, while increasing thrust from 640,000 to 715,000 pounds-force," Blue Origin said in a second blog post today. "Manufacturing advancements have also significantly shortened production timelines, enabling assembly in less than a day."

Northstar went from program approval to development testing in just 11 months, according to the company. But today's update did not say when the new engine will fly for the first time.

New York's New AI Law Prohibits Bots From Dangerous Behaviors With Minors

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Starting in January, New Yorkers can file complaints with the attorney general if chatbots cross a line with their kids.

Artificial intelligence is everywhere now. It has become part of most Americans’ work and personal lives at an unprecedented pace, popping up in our inboxes and social media apps even when we haven’t opted into a chatbot ourselves. Examples of the harm have rolled in, from staggering numbers of deepfake nudes being created of children to teens who turn to AI for advice during a mental health crisis. But in New York, new legislation will try to rein in the technology and the risks it poses to minors.

The new law, which takes effect on Jan. 1, 2027, prohibits companies with AI chatbots “from offering unsafe features to minors, including chatbots that pretend to be human, build emotional dependency, encourage self-harm or engage in sexually explicit conversations.” Companies would have to block these features on the accounts of anyone under 18, or any user whose age has not been verified. The law requires chatbot providers to verify users through multiple age assurance methods.

Once verified, that age information must be deleted. It was signed into law by Gov. Kathy Hochul on Oct. 28, 2026.

According to Gov. Hochul’s website, the legislation prohibits AI chatbots from, among other things:

Violations of the new law could result in fines of up to $25,000 per infraction. The attorney general will publish a new website where New Yorkers can file complaints. New York’s new law is an attempt to curtail the behaviors that are most harmful to kids, experts say.

The story of Adam Raine — a teenager who died by suicide, and whose conversations with ChatGPT exposed that it offered to write his suicide note for him — is one example of why humanizing chatbot behaviors like these are targeted by this law. Surveys have shown kids ages 11 to 16 can’t really articulate what AI is, and many ascribe human-like consciousness and emotions to chatbots.

“Children are developing these relationships because they are vulnerable to do so. It’s not enough for companies to just put in guardrails for something that happens after the fact. What they’re asking companies to do is to understand the risks and build according to what we believe those risks are,” says Dona Fraser, SVP of Privacy Initiatives at BBB National Programs, an independent industry accountability organization. “We need to be able to distinguish between the beneficial uses of AI and the design features that create these unnecessary risks.”

According to a 2026 Common Sense Media report, nearly 9 in 10 kids ages 9 to 17 (86%) interact with AI, including about one in four who use it on a daily basis. More than half of kids (57%) who use AI said they’ve used it to get information about their health or body; separate studies have found that AI gives misleading or inaccurate health information to users 50% of the time. Half of the respondents have asked AI for advice on decisions about their future or goals, while 37% have used chatbots to discuss their feelings or personal problems.

As always, legislation lumbers along while technology speeds ahead unchecked. Until the laws catch up to AI, parents can feel alone in the fight to protect their kids. Fraser says this law on its own is not enough to protect them either.

“Legislation alone cannot guarantee safety. Companies have got to do their part, and there has got to be accountability,” she says. “The Meta settlements are a great example of what industry self-regulation looks like. It doesn’t work. There’s got to be third-party independent accountability because companies, for all sorts of reasons, can’t always take five steps back to see what it is that they’re doing. Sometimes they can't see the risks that are right in front of them. So I do think that the law is a step in the right direction. We need these types of laws, but we also need the companies to do their part.”

The White House is apparently a popular residential proxy exit spot

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

How it got there is another matter entirely

Whether through malware or other means, devices associated with the White House network appear to be serving as residential proxy exit nodes, potentially posing security risks to one of the world's most sensitive networks.

News of the security snafu comes from security firm Layer3 Intel, which reported this week that it discovered the likely illicit activity thanks to its recently launched Residential Proxy Observatory. According to Layer3 founder Sergiy Usatyuk, the White House residential proxy activity might have gone unnoticed if not for the new tool.

“The observatory is just a public interface to explore the data we've been collecting for the past year,” Usatyuk told The Register in an email.

For those unfamiliar with residential proxies, they're like any old proxy network that obfuscates the location of a user, except in this case their exit nodes use IP addresses associated with residential internet connections, as opposed to datacenters. While many are legitimate, others are exploited by threat actors and used in the commission of cybercrimes, as the FBI warned earlier this year.

The FBI bulletin, put out in March, warns users to protect their systems against hijacking via malware, illicit SDK partnerships, VPNs with hidden service terms that repurpose user computers as exit nodes, and other threats used to turn victims into nodes without their consent.

Residential proxies are commonly used by threat actors to conceal command-and-control (C2) infrastructure, conduct phishing attacks, send spam, disguise malicious traffic, and the like, the FBI explained.

According to Layer3, its data shows 28 distinct residential proxy services exiting into the White House network nearly every day between October 5 and November 3, with 236 separate exits in that period. Other residential proxy detection services logged the same information, Layer3 said.

Most of the activity was happening in the evening, White House time, between 5 pm and midnight. That, Layer3 said, suggests someone at the White House - not a guest - has an infected device that’s serving as an exit, likely via an infected proxy SDK. If correct, that’s a problem.

“Past research has shown that poorly secured proxy SDKs can allow internal network access and lateral movement,” Layer3 explained. “If an SDK on this network has the same kind of flaw, anyone buying proxy access could use it to reach internal systems.”

In other words, this is a serious security risk, and one Usatyuk tells us could have been a problem for a while.

“Our data retention period goes back to July 10,” the Layer3 founder explained. “Given that there was continuous activity for a matter of months and other res proxy detection services independently verified the data, it's likely that White House IPs have been used by res proxy networks for all sorts of activity for some time now.”

Usatyuk said his firm’s data isn’t detailed enough to know how many machines are being used as exit nodes inside the White House network. He pointed us to an X post from security firm Synthient, which said it believes there are multiple Windows 10 and 11 devices on the White House network that likely have the Infatica SDK installed on them.

Infatica offers a software development kit (SDK) that allows app developers to monetize users' internet connections by turning their devices into residential proxy exit nodes. The possibility that this software is running on devices associated with the White House network raises serious security concerns: Vulnerable proxy software could potentially provide attackers with a route into internal systems.

Usatyuk told us that the most recent residential proxy activity on the White House network occurred Wednesday night, “hours after the blog went live,” but has since stopped. The US State Department contacted Layer3 Thursday morning to request a copy of the company’s logs, which Usatyuk tells us he’s sent over.

“Hopefully they've figured out the situation,” Usatyuk added - and hopefully before any government secrets have been exposed.

The White House didn't respond to questions for this story.®

Ballot measures affirm divisions over abortion and transgender issues

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

Theresa covers substance use and addiction and co-writes the Morning Rounds newsletter. You can reach Theresa on Signal at theresagaff.97.

Jonathan Wosen is STAT’s West Coast biotech & life sciences reporter. You can reach Jonathan on Signal at jwosen.27.

The blue wave that handed Democrats power in both the House and Senate did not filter neatly through ballot measures across the country.

Measures on abortion, marijuana, science funding, and Medicaid largely reflected a more liberal national sentiment. But multiple states voted to implement or enhance restrictions on transgender youth.

In Arizona, Colorado, and Washington, voters approved or were poised to approve measures to enact or expand prohibitions on transgender youth participation on school athletics teams aligned with their gender identity. Colorado also was on track to approve a new ban on gender-affirming surgery for minors.

While critics called the measures on transgender rights “ballot candy” for Republicans, there was no correlation between the measures and winning candidates’ affiliations. Colorado Democrats, for example, won the Senate, most House seats, and both the governor and attorney general offices.

The results indicate that many voters are concerned about gender, sports, and fairness in particular, but those worries may be outsized compared to the number of young transgender people these laws will apply to. Existing data suggest around 300,000 youth ages 13 to 17 nationally identify as transgender, and 40% may participate in sports. In late 2024, the NCAA said that there were fewer than 10 transgender athletes among more than 500,000 total. And in response to a recent poll, an NCAA representative confirmed that zero transgender athletes currently compete in women’s leagues.

Data is limited and murky on the physical advantage that transgender women may have in various competitions, but research so far does not suggest any inherent edge. When it comes to medical care, data show that top surgery is rare among minors, while bottom surgery on a minor’s genitals is already virtually nonexistent.

The Trump administration has relentlessly targeted youth gender-affirming care in the last two years, leading many hospitals to stop providing care like puberty blockers and hormones, even in states where the care is still legal. It’s unclear what that fight will look like now, as candidates like Abdul El-Sayed and James Talarico, who have expressed support for transgender youth, won across the country. Locally, six transgender candidates won seats in state legislatures that have never previously elected transgender representatives.

“The voters rejected the anti-trans rhetoric used against candidates while still stripping away our rights at the ballot,” Chase Strangio, who leads the ACLU’s LGBT & HIV Project, said on Instagram. Strangio argued that ballot measures are “a function of funding more than anything” and should not be used as justification for more restrictive legislation.

The blue wave could have trickle-down effects on Colorado’s ballot measures. Both proposals are statutory changes, which means that even once they officially pass, the state legislature can make amendments before enacting them. On Wednesday, the state’s governor-elect said the restrictions would need “a long, hard look,” before implementation, The Denver Post reported.

Four states voted on ballot measures related to abortion, and all preserved the existing legal status.

The most contentious vote was in Missouri, where just two years ago voters narrowly approved a measure to enshrine the right to reproductive freedom in the constitution. The state legislature asked voters to reverse that decision, tying a potential abortion ban to a more popular proposal to constitutionally ban gender-affirming care for minors. (That care is already illegal in the state, but not constitutionally prohibited.) Voters rejected the bid, with nearly 56% of the vote against it.

In Nevada and Virginia, where abortion is already legal, strong majorities voted to enshrine that right in the state constitutions. Four years after the Supreme Court overruled Roe v. Wade, 26 states have some type of legal protections on abortion.

But stalwart red Idaho denied the right to abortion, preserving the state’s existing ban.

A measure to repeal Massachusetts’ recreational marijuana law was defeated handily, with nearly 76% of voters choosing to keep the grass growing. Polling showed the measure had little chance of passing, yet out of the state’s nine ballot questions, it became the most expensive. The measure was almost entirely funded by Smart Approaches to Marijuana, a national nonprofit that advocates against legalization that put $3.7 million into the campaign. Opponents paid $2.8 million.

While no other states considered measures to legalize drugs like marijuana or psychedelics, Idaho voters preserved their right to do so in the future. More than 66% rejected a proposal that would have given all power to legalize psychoactive drugs to the legislature.

But results on other drug policy measures seem to complicate the narrative of a big blue wave. Voters in two states approved measures that appear to endorse the Trump administration’s punitive approach to drug policy from his executive order naming fentanyl as a weapon of mass destruction. Arizona approved a measure naming drug cartels terrorist organizations and directing the state’s Department of Homeland Security to address the threat, while Colorado voted to increase criminal penalties for the possession, manufacture, and sale of fentanyl. (Colorado’s governor-elect said this, too, may need to be reconsidered.)

Golden State voters appeared on track to pass California’s Proposition 38, which authorizes $8.4 billion in bonds to fund immunology and immunotherapy research. The result suggests that the state’s voters were eager to defend research funding after the Trump administration’s slew of grant terminations, delays, and disruptions. But the measure has generated controversy because of where that funding will likely go.

Prop 38 will direct half the funds, $4.2 billion, to a single institute that isn’t named explicitly but which must meet a long list of criteria that experts in California politics call “oddly specific.” Only one institute appears to meet those criteria: the California Institute for Immunology & Immunotherapy. This institute, also known as CIII, was cofounded by Gary Michelson, a billionaire philanthropist, retired surgeon, and Prop 38’s leading backer. Michelson poured more than $40 million of his own money into the Yes on 38 campaign; fellow CIII co-founder and treasure Meyer Luskin contributed $5 million in support of the measure. As of mid-October, the “No on 38” campaign had raised less than $9,000.

CIII is still under construction and went from having zero employees during the 2024 calendar year to three last month, according to documents filed with the Internal Revenue Service and reporting by CalMatters. STAT’s own reporting revealed that the American Association of Immunologists, the field’s leading scientific society, seriously considered pulling its endorsement of Prop 38 once it became clear half the funds would likely go to Michelson’s institute. The organization decided to maintain support after its governing body met with Michelson in August.

The measure also drew support from more than 70 organizations, including Gov. Gavin Newsom and the state’s Democratic and Republican parties.

Proponents argued that this investment would help researchers working to cure or treat new treatments for Alzheimer’s, heart disease, and cancer. They also framed Prop 38 as a way for voters to protect California from abrupt turns in federal science funding.

Other health-related ballot initiatives saw mixed results, reflecting voters’ overarching concern with affordability and funding.

As national Medicaid cuts loom, South Dakota voters rejected an amendment that would have conditioned the state’s expansion program — already approved by voters — on the federal share of funding remaining at 90%. Proponents of the measure argued that decreases in federal funding would make it too expensive for the state. But it also would have meant a sudden loss of coverage for the tens of thousands of residents who are enrolled through the expansion program.

Arizona voters approved a measure to require voter approval for any local taxes on groceries, and to cap any such tax at 2%. (There’s no statewide sales tax on groceries, but local jurisdictions can enact them.)

North Dakota voters narrowly rejected a proposed constitutional amendment to require public schools to offer all students free breakfast and lunch. The program would have cost $124.3 million to $134.3 million every two years, per the North Dakota Monitor.

STAT’s coverage of chronic health issues is supported by a grant from Bloomberg Philanthropies. Our financial supporters are not involved in any decisions about our journalism.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Blue Origin plans to do its next New Glenn rocket launch in January 2027, according to CNBC and the Financial Times, which cite a memo from CEO Dave Limp sent to staff. The new timeline follows a May launchpad explosion that damaged Blue Origin’s New Glenn launchpad ahead of a flight that would have brought Amazon Leo satellites into space.

In October, Blue Origin founder Jeff Bezos told Fox News that the company was aiming for a December New Glenn launch, joking that it could be “probably December 31st.” The new, revised launch window is “about 10 days later than Blue Origin’s earlier launch target,” CNBC reports. Limp also reportedly cited bad weather as having an effect on the launchpad rebuilding process.

“This small adjustment doesn’t diminish what we’ve accomplished or the importance of the work still ahead,” Limp wrote in his memo, per CNBC and the FT. “Returning New Glenn to flight remains our top priority — it’s worth it.”

Los Angeles Mayor-Elect Nithya Raman Vows To Triple Homebuilding To Improve Affordability

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Los Angeles City Councilmember Nithya Raman has unseated the city's Democrat incumbent Mayor Karen Bass after running a campaign centered on affordability and housing.

The Associated Press called the election for Raman on Thursday afternoon as she widened her lead over Bass.

Bass, 73, called Raman to concede her loss and congratulate her successor on her victory.

"While this is not the outcome we hoped for, the people of Los Angeles have spoken," the outgoing mayor said in a statement, in part. "I have committed to ensuring a smooth and seamless transition. Moving this city forward is what Los Angeles needs from all of us."

The win underscored a "blue wave" that swept Democrats into control of both chambers of Congress, delivering a decisive rebuke to President Donald Trump and the Republican Party.

When Raman, 45, takes office in January, L.A. will become the nation's second-largest city led by a democratic socialist, following in the footsteps of New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani.

Raman's swearing-in will expand a growing roster of democratic socialist mayors nationwide, including Janeese Lewis George in Washington, DC, Katie Wilson in Seattle, and David Morales, in Providence, RI.

Ahead of her victory, Raman appeared before a jubilant crowd of supporters at the Greek Theatre Plaza in Griffith Park Tuesday, arguing that L.A. voters were frustrated with Bass' record on cost of living and were demanding change, reported KTLA5.

"We watched friend after friend, family after family, leave because they couldn’t afford to stay here anymore. And we all talked about it as if these things were completely out of City Hall’s control," she told the audience. "That’s the cynicism that started to take over this city. That started to make us believe that L.A.’s problems weren't just bad, but they were permanent."

Raman ran a campaign focusing on housing, homelessness, and safety issues. The centerpiece of her platform was a plan to triple annual housing production by speeding up permitting through strict "shot clocks" for approvals.

Further proposals included overhauling inspections, updating codes, and eliminating additional fees to incentivize developers to build more starter homes.

""oday, less than 10% of the new homes built in L.A. are for-sale," Raman recently told Realtor.com®. "Under current city and state regulations, for-sale projects are charged higher fees, have a more-complicated approval process, are easier to appeal, and have increased liability risks for developers."

The 43-year-old councilmember was sharply critical of her mayoral predecessor, contending that Bass has not done enough to tackle L.A.’s yearslong housing crisis.

Bass’ record includes signing Executive Directive 1, which streamlined approval for roughly 50,000 units of fully affordable housing, overseeing a historic rezoning program to clear a path for building half a million new homes, adding more housing units to transit corridors and job hubs, simplifying the building application process, and updating rent stabilization rules for the first time in 40 years to shield tenants from steep hikes.

However, it was on Bass’ watch that Realtor.com Metro Report Cards released in September ranked L.A. the nation’s least affordable large city—a result that Raman did not find surprising.

"We’ve made it too difficult, time consuming, and costly to build homes in Los Angeles," concluded Raman.

Assuming a 10% down payment and a 6.5% 30-year fixed mortgage, a median earner in L.A. would have to spend nearly 85% of their income on monthly mortgage payments on a $1.12 million median-priced home, effectively putting ownership out of reach of all but the wealthiest buyers.

Beyond cost-of-living issues, Raman face pressure upon taking office to deliver on her lofty campaign pledge to reduce citywide homelessness by 50% in time for the 2028 Summer Olympics in Los Angeles.

Fulfilling that promise will be a major challenge, given that recent 2026 data showed roughly 45,000 people living in tents and encampments on the streets of L.A.

Snejana Farberov is a reporter at Realtor.com covering the U.S. housing market and the latest domestic real estate trends. She has worked as a general assignment journalist in New York City and Long Island for 16 years, writing for New York Post, Daily Mail, and News 12. Snejana earned bachelor's degrees in journalism and Italian from St. John's University, followed by a master’s degree from Columbia University School of Journalism.

Meeks vows Iran votes as he seeks Foreign Affairs gavel

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Rep. Gregory W. Meeks, D-N.Y., the presumed next chairman of the House Foreign Affairs Committee, plans to bring resolutions to the floor to “immediately” end the war with Iran when Democrats take control of the House in January, he wrote in a letter Thursday seeking his colleagues’ support to become chairman.

The letter, which officially announced his bid for the chairmanship, lays out his oversight agenda for the committee, which will also see considerable turnover on the GOP side of the dais.

Meeks and other Democrats have repeatedly forced votes this year on war powers resolutions seeking to end the Iran war, with a Meeks-sponsored one clearing both chambers of Congress.

But those resolutions have been nonbinding concurrent resolutions, since the 1973 War Powers Resolution only lays out a fast-track procedure the minority party can wield for concurrent resolutions in the House.

With a House majority, though, Democrats could bring binding joint resolutions to the floor.

Beyond war powers votes, Meeks also promised public hearings on the war. He also vowed hearings on a range of topics including the administration’s Venezuela policy and “repeated defiance of Congress’s constitutional authority over war powers and trade.”

“As chairman in the 120th Congress, oversight of this administration’s lawlessness and corruption will be at the top of the agenda,” Meeks wrote. “I will continue to advance a principled foreign policy grounded in our values, including support for Ukraine against Russian aggression.”

In addition to Iran measures, Meeks also promised legislation focused on “clawing back Congress’s proper role in the making of American foreign policy” and a State Department reauthorization bill that puts guardrails on the administration and protects career diplomats.

Meanwhile, the GOP side of the committee is poised to lose at least a dozen lawmakers — or about half of its members — next year between retirements and election losses.

The departures include longtime members such as former Chairman Michael McCaul, R-Texas, who opted not to seek reelection, and Rep. Scott Perry, R-Pa., the Freedom Caucus chair who lost his reelection race.

Several other Republican committee members remained locked in races that were still too close to call as of Thursday afternoon, according to The Associated Press, potentially gutting the GOP committee bench further. They include GOP Rep. Young Kim of California, the East Asia and Pacific Subcommittee chair who was trailing her in race against fellow California Republican Rep. Ken Calvert.

Rep. Christopher H. Smith, R-N.J., who chairs the Africa Subcommittee, lamented the turnover Thursday but predicted a lot of interest among remaining House Republicans to fill the void.

“There will be many who will want to be on it,” Smith told reporters. “We lost some really great people. Yesterday I can’t tell you how sad I was about friends who are now not coming back.”

DOJ Creates 'Super Intelligence' Task Force to Guard Against AI Abuse

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

DOJ Creates 'Super Intelligence' Task Force to Guard Against AI Abuse

The U.S. Department of Justice is setting up a task force dedicated to policing the misuse of advanced artificial intelligence, as Washington races to build enforcement muscle around a technology it increasingly frames in near-sci-fi terms.

Announced Thursday, the Super Intelligence Task Force, or DOJ-SITF, will be led by Associate Attorney General Stanley Woodward and Associate Deputy Attorney General Stephen Weiss.

Its stated mission is to make sure American leadership in “super intelligence” isn’t compromised by bad actors, whether foreign governments and their proxies, transnational criminal organizations, or companies and individuals using AI to commit fraud or other crimes.

The department invoked President Donald Trump’s framing that “America stands at the forefront of a new technological revolution in intelligence.”

“SI development will continue to be powered by American innovators,” Attorney General Todd Blanche said in a statement, adding that the task force would work alongside SI Force Chair Jay Clayton to keep critical AI technologies from being “manipulated by bad actors at home or abroad.”

Blanche said the DOJ would keep “identifying and prosecuting individuals whose use of SI violates the law, including by maliciously targeting American taxpayers, critical U.S. infrastructure, or other aspects of our national security.”

Notably, the effort isn’t purely about catching criminals. Weiss said the department also has “a responsibility” to “harden regular reporting on SI risk and response, and ensure companies exercise vigorous internal controls so that SI technology behaves as intended,” signaling that the DOJ wants AI developers themselves to tighten safeguards against models going off the rails.

The term “super intelligence” typically refers to a hypothetical AI that surpasses human capability across the board, and the government’s embrace of the phrase marks an escalation in how officials talk about the technology. Former SEC chair Jay Clayton has been tied to a broader Trump administration push around super intelligence, and the SEC recently added “super intelligence” to its 2027 examination priorities.

The task force joins a widening federal scramble to get ahead of AI risk. Trump signed an executive order setting up a fight with states over AI regulation. Earlier this week, the U.S. banking regulator OCC issued its first AI framework as banks battle rogue agents reaching into customer accounts.

The DOJ did not specify a timeline or detail how the task force would coordinate with other agencies, saying only that its work would “promote innovation and prevent the misuse” of the technology.

BMW bumps battery for the better: The 2027 i7 60 xDrive, driven

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

BMW has updated its flagship sedan with the newest EV and infotainment tech.

When BMW debuted the new 7 Series in 2022, it set a new standard for large luxury sedans, increasingly irrelevant though that class is. Rather than siloing off an electric alternative like Mercedes, with its EQS alongside the normal S-Class, BMW simply made the powertrain an option. Stick with a V8 if you want, or consider the rather good electric version instead—only subtle differences like the badging and lack of exhaust pipes give the game away.

Since then, BMW has brought out a string of even newer electric vehicles for the 5 and 3 Series (plus their SUV counterparts). It has switched from battery packs made of prismatic cells to more energy-dense arrangements of cylindrical cells. The motors are still electrically excited synchronous ones, but now the drive units feature silicon carbide inverters and more compact packaging.

This sixth-generation electric powertrain was designed for the clean-sheet Neue Klasse family, which also threw out the legacy cruft of dozens and dozens of discrete single-function electronic control units for a handful of powerful domain controllers. These computers take over those functions but with much faster processing power—and therefore more capabilities in terms of chassis control or powertrain efficiency—as well as the ability to be more easily updated.

Which could have left BMW with something of a problem—should the flagship be clearly outclassed in terms of power and energy density and range efficiency by, well, all the other EVs in the range? Clearly, the board decided the answer to that was no, and for the 7 Series’ midlife update, the i7 got some comprehensive surgery under the skin.

Compared to the last i7 I drove, the 2027 model boasts a higher-capacity battery pack, now 112.5 kWh (net) up from 101.7 kWh, although BMW stuck with a 400 V architecture, unlike in the Neue Klasse or the iX5 we recently drove. A less-powerful 50 xDrive and a more powerful M70 xDrive will also be available, but at the first drive, I sampled the midrange 60 xDrive model, which features a combined 544 hp (400 KW) and 549 lb-ft (745 Nm), roughly equivalent to the pre-facelift car.

Despite that power and torque, the i7 60 doesn’t feel fast like a Porsche Taycan might, and you won’t want to hustle it down a mountain road the way you might the Porsche. The entire driving experience is waftier than that, with the car offering more of a magic carpet ride, a la the iX SUV or even that very fancy cousin of the i7, the Rolls-Royce Spectre.

Even with launch control, 0–60 mph takes 4.6 seconds. Once upon a time, this would have been considered excellent—the V12 750i of the mid-1990s needed at least two seconds more to do that job. But 30 years later, electric sedans from Lucid and Tesla and Porsche and a whole load of Chinese automakers will get you there in less than half the i7’s time, should you want that. If you’re looking for an outright speed machine that will rearrange your internal organs under acceleration, look elsewhere.

In fact, the i7 was remarkably relaxed; even with the traction and stability control turned down, it refused to chirp a wheel or even threaten to break loose in the dry at speeds considered acceptable on the road. But drive it in a more relaxed manner, and it rewards. The steering is precise, and it coasts beautifully on flat or downhill grades, a consequence of those EESMs that offer no resistance to the road when they’re not receiving a charge. The same very smooth regenerative braking to a stop that we experienced in the iX3 and iX5 shows up in the i7, too.

First drive events are never a good way to determine range efficiency, but BMW says between 344—362 miles depending on wheel size. With only a 400 V pack, DC fast-charging tops out at 250 kW (up from 195 kW), and a 10–80 percent charge takes about 28 minutes. Like other model-year 2027 BMW EVs, the i7 has a NACS charging port.

The domain controller for the powertrain also helps efficiency by working with the route-planning software. BMW also runs AI simulations of the battery based on previous charging cycles when planning routes and picking charging stops along the destination and will even take the cost per kWH at different stations into account.

There was also some rather radical surgery in the cabin, with BMW’s new Panoramic iDrive screen running along the base of the windshield, and its new trapezoidal infotainment touchscreen replacing the older system that was inspired by boutique hotel interiors.

BMW also put in a new domain controller for the infotainment systems. The front passenger screen and theater screen that folds down from the roof for the rear passengers operate independently of each other and the main infotainment screen—logging in to YouTube or Disney+ on one will not log you in on the others, for instance. Watching content on the theater screen in the back is also a little more enticing in the abstract than the practical.

The 31.3-inch touchscreen boasts 8K resolution, but because of the aspect ratio of the screen, if you’re watching a movie—or perhaps F1 qualifying—it will only fill the middle third of the screen, with big black bars on either side. Plus, you have to tilt your head back quite a bit to look up at the screen. The Dolby Atmos sound system was impressive to my relatively cloth ears, though.

While BMW has increased the processing power, there was still some noticeable lag on the main infotainment screen. The Alexa-based voice recognition software was notably better than when I encountered it a few months ago in the iX3, however.

The new i7 range starts at $106,200 for the 50 xDrive, or $124,700 for the 60 xDrive.

With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

An Everyman’s Guide to Wearing a Necklace

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

You already have a watch you like. This is the next step in your jewelry journey.

Men’s jewelry, I’ve learned, can be a slippery slope. I’ve been a watch guy since elementary school, if we’re generous enough to count a dual-sided compass/watch on a carabiner as a watch. In college I added a bracelet. I’ve been a ring guy for over ten years, thanks mostly to being married and partially to getting lunch drunk in Albuquerque, going ring shopping for my wife, and finding a sterling silver bear signet ring that I’ve worn nonstop since. I’ve had an earring for two years, thanks to a quick weekend in Miami and a well-timed Harrison Ford interview clip crossing my algorithm about getting his ear pierced in a Claire’s.

Recently, I’ve been curious about my personal final frontier of men’s jewelry—the necklace. The other listings above are all fairly low entry points, safe experiments in the world of men’s jewelry. The earring may be the exception, but this is 2026; one small silver hoop isn’t that daring, even in the Midwest. A necklace has never really been my thing. I haven’t felt the pull. That is, until this year.

For an unrelated project, I was breaking down some of the signature stylistic calling cards of Robert Redford. And there it was, across paparazzi pictures and movie stills and in memoriam roundups. In almost every photo, Redford has some kind of necklace, usually peeking from a precariously unbuttoned western shirt.

Further study of what we can safely call Hollywood’s golden age of off-duty style shows the same: McQueen and Newman and their contemporaries, swanning around on and off film, always wearing some sort of necklace or chain or pendant.

This is, of course, a common trap: to look at a famously handsome Hollywood star and think, yeah, all that’s separating me from THAT is a necklace. And sure, a good cheekbone and clavicle will get you pretty far in life. There’s no magic amulet here, nothing that will shazam you into leading-man looks. But there are a few lessons on styling from guys whose literal job it was to present themselves favorably to the world. And a few lessons on what necklaces to choose to help get there.

So, I put my professional online shopping style writing gig into overdrive, going deep into the Getty Images archives and confusing Google’s settings by repeatedly searching “Insert Hollywood Icon + shirtless” to glean some jewelry 101 tips from the professionals.

Instagram and (theoretically, since I don’t indulge) TikTok are full of maximalizing style trend chasers. Why wear one necklace when you can wear four? Why two rings when each finger could have two rings on it? Why dip your toes in the water when you can cannonball into the deep end? And sure, there’s some je ne sais quoi about just committing to something fully and completely out of the gate. But there’s also a lot of try-hard to it.

We’re channeling a more reserved, less #sponsoredpost era of dressing here that cares less about link clicks and more about how things look in the real world. So start simple and start with a chain. My favorite, and currently on daily rotation, is the Curb Chain from L.A.-based Bloedstone. Mine is sterling silver and in the inch length, which hits closer to the sternum than the neck. That’s the prescribed Hollywood A-lister way to wear it, but chart your own path if you want to. Miansai has a thicker 2mm Cuban chain that’s great too. And while we’re covering the M’s, Mejuri has one too. To my slight surprise, my home state watchmaker Shinola makes one, and less surprisingly so, Esquire-approved David Yurman has this giant one featuring a marketing campaign with Michael B. Jordan, who is picking up McQueen’s (and subsequently Brosnan’s) torch by adapting The Thomas Crown Affair.

There’s a whole vocabulary on link types and thickness and clasps out there. Don’t worry about that for now. Find something that calls to you and click add to cart. We’re starting slowly here.

Parting note. There are a lot of chains worn over T-shirts these days. To each their own, but the leading man’s move is to keep it under your shirt. If you want to show it off, just ditch the buttons.

A chain is simple, and sure, the greats wore them. But more often than not, they had something dangling from that chain. Medallion, pendant, a bottle opener in Cool Hand Luke. McQueen was often photographed, including in the pages of Esquire, wearing a St. Christopher medallion. Seeing as he’s the patron saint of travelers, St. Christopher medallions are having a bit of a cultural resurgence, partially because of their traditional association as a good-luck charm for travelers. There are some fun enamel ones on eBay, including these surfing-specific ones. Every once in a while, Nashville-based Americana experts Imogene + Willie will pull together a vintage edit of St. Christopher medallions that always sell out in around 24 hours. Speaking of Nashville, if you’re going to go new, go with Yearly Co. The gold jewelry experts have a 14k version of the iconography with a pitch-perfect 24-inch chain. I can attest to the quality—I gave my wife the brand’s diamond huggies and she wears them almost every day. As the grandson of a Protestant minister, I steer away from the saints out of genetic loyalty to Luther’s 95 Theses, but I recently picked up this Perseverance medallion from the brand, inspired by a vintage signet ring. It scratches the McQueen itch with flying colors.

Secondhand gold is also an option. Alloy Market recently came onto my radar, and I’m browsing it daily for myself and gifts for my wife. They’ve got a small but mighty collection of certified men’s items with pendants and medallions you can mix and match. All of the quality without any of the markup.

Don’t limit yourself to just a medallion, though. Get a little funky. Redford did for a while with this turquoise number that somehow looked even cooler hitting under the sternum. Shinola’s got an option there too. Bali-based Salty Dagger has an Odysseus-approved Greek mythology piece. And why not try a locket, like this one from Pattaraphan. Miansai has a great pendant. Get fancy with the Prevaut diamond ring deal. When in doubt, steal from the girls. I got this With Clarity birthstone necklace for my wife that I might take a swing at myself.

Peyote Bird High Desert Sterling Silver Necklace

Shinola 21mm Turquoise Inlay Tag Necklace

From the abbreviated/slightly bastardized Shakespeare: “All the world’s a stage and one man in his time plays many parts.” Jewelry can feel like a risk, even if you’re following some sort of contrived guide to styling and buying it. Sometimes we pretend until we’re comfortable actually being. Redford and McQueen and company were actors. Pretending was their legacy, and they were good at it. Take a leaf from their book and get dressed for the curtain call.

Inside the Underground Mosques of Kazakhstan’s Surreal Mangystau Desert

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Up until about 20 years ago, a faint trail up a steep hill through limestone-and-chalk cliffs was the only way to reach Beket-Ata, one of the sacred underground mosques—as they’re colloquially known—of Kazakhstan’s Mangystau desert. “There was no proper path then, but people made the difficult climb in the heat and wind,” my guide, Yerzhan Nagmanov, tells me as we embark on our way to the holy site—eased slightly by the stairs now carved into the stone. That arduous journey, the travelers believed, “was part of the pilgrimage.”

En route to Beket-Ata with Mangystau Tour 999, visitors walk a trail cutting through the steep hill.

The trail of stairs descending into the canyon en route to Beket-Ata.

As inhospitable as the roughly 64,000-square-mile desert may seem, it has attracted scores of people over the years arriving to see Beket-Ata and the four other sacred complexes scattered across its expanse: Shopan-Ata, Karaman-Ata, Shakpak-Ata and Sultan-Epe. Traveling between the five holy sites, the distinction between architecture and geology evaporates into the desert heat: Walls, ceilings, and floors are all variations of the rock from which the mosques were excavated. For pilgrims, the journey to these sacred sites is an act of devotion, where people come to honor revered Sufi masters, pray for blessings, and seek spiritual guidance in times of difficulty.

The mosques are hundreds of years old, collectively dating between the 12th and 18th centuries, but they’re new to the UNESCO World Heritage List as of this summer. The “Rock Mosques and Associated Sacred Sites of Mangystau” were added to the esteemed collection of landmarks in July.

Each mosque is associated with a different spiritual master within Sufism, the mystical branch of Islam. The five Sufi masters’ influence shaped the region’s religious landscape, and then the landscape itself, as all of the mosques—crafted in a rock-cut style that grew out of nomadic building traditions—seem to grow directly from the desert terrain; they’re carved into caves, tucked along the contours of cliffs and hillsides, and tunneled below the ground.

After three weeks travelling through Kazakhstan’s cities, mountains, and pine forests, I spent my final week in Mangystau, a landscape so different from anything I’d seen so far in the country that it felt like a whole world unto itself. Having recently visited Astana’s Grand Mosque, with its blue domes, soaring minarets, and gleaming expanses of marble, I wondered whether Mangystau’s rock-cut shrines might fall flat by comparison. For me, however, their lack of splashy grandeur is precisely what made them so compelling.

A stately arch welcomes travelers to the steps leading to Beket-Ata.

While I am not Muslim, I was fascinated by how the Sufi masters traversed this unforgiving terrain along with their families. Some walked hundreds of kilometers to establish places of worship far from the comforts of the world, spending years in prayer and contemplation before eventually being buried here. I was drawn to the way the rock mosques embody a humble yet stunning expression of faith shaped by the austere landscape, where instead of gilded shrines or massive palaces, devotion found a more organic form. Through guided 4x4 tours with Red Maya Travel and Mangystau Tour 999, I visited all five.

A necropolis inside the Shopan-Ata complex.

My first stop on the tour is Shopan-Ata, named after the 12th-century Sufi teacher. The complex consists of a vast necropolis (a large burial ground with tombs of religious figures and generations of pilgrims) of more than 2,000 monuments, including domed mausoleums and kulpytas, which are ornately carved stone steles traditionally used as Kazakh gravestones. The 12-chamber mosque is carved into the rock, and a shaft of daylight is cast onto two sacred wooden poles below. According to a local tradition, pilgrims recite a prayer and circle the poles three times counterclockwise, which is believed to help the spirits hear them. Visitors also tie strips of white cloth around the poles, the knot symbolically binding a prayer to the sacred site. These practices likely come from Shamanic and Tengrian traditions of tying strips of cloth to sacred trees and mountain passes to honor nature spirits––customs that became intertwined with local forms of Muslim saint veneration over time.

Nearby, a well offers pilgrims a much-needed respite; these sacred complexes grew around the few natural resources that made life in the desert possible.

Domed mausoleums at the Shopan-Ata necropolis.

The entrance to Shopan-Ata, shaded by a mulberry tree. These mosques were built around oases that sustained life in the harsh desert climate.

Legend holds that centuries later, Shopan-Ata appeared to Beket-Ata in a dream, offering spiritual guidance, making this shrine an important stop before continuing to Beket-Ata. Nearby, a well offers pilgrims a much-needed respite, its water an incongruous presence in a landscape this dry––a reminder that these sacred complexes grew around the few natural resources that made life in the desert possible.

The sacred wooden pole, around which visitors tie white cloth to seek blessings.

The final few stairs leading up to the Beket-Ata shrine.

Just over an hour away, Beket-Ata is perhaps the best-known of the five mosques. Founded by the 18th-century Sufi teacher Beket Myrzagululy, the shrine lies in a remote canyon about 170 miles from Aktau, the Caspian-side gateway to Mangystau. The steep descent into the canyon is a reminder that the effort needed to reach these shrines has always been a part of the devotion.

The structure is carved into the pale rock, its interconnected chambers following the curves of the cliff. Narrow openings in the ceilings pull shafts of daylight into the cool, dim rooms, where rugs are spread across the stone floor.

Communal dining is integral to Kazakh culture.

An imam is usually on hand to receive visitors and share information about the site. As we settle on the carpets, he details each of the chambers, one of which contains Beket-Ata’s grave, and another is associated with his sister. The remaining rooms are used for prayer, with separate spaces for men and women.

My pilgrimage continues into the dining hall, where a long, continually replenished table runs through the room, with pilgrims sitting on either side and eating together. The mosque provides some freshly cooked meat free of charge, while visitors leave bread, sweets, tea, yogurt, jam and other provisions for those who arrive after them—an especially vivid expression of Central Asian hospitality.

Next we drive across remote country to the Tyub-Karagan Peninsula for Shakpak-Ata, whose cruciform mosque is carved into a massive chalk cliff. Its porous white surface, which looks almost like honeycomb against the desert, was shaped millions of years ago when this part of Mangystau lay beneath the ancient Tethys Sea. Inside, drawings and inscriptions in Arabic, Persian, and Turkic—some dating back to the 18th century—offer a tangible record of the pilgrims who traveled here from across Central Asia, long before air-conditioned 4x4s and Starlink internet made traversing this unforgiving landscape simpler.

A honeycomb-like pattern along the rock face of Shakpak-Ata.

Slightly west, Sultan-Epe lies beside a deep canyon, where a stream threads through thickets of mulberry, buckthorn, blackberry, and wild herbs—another improbable pocket of green in one of Kazakhstan’s driest regions. Sultan-Epe is revered as a protector of seafarers and fishermen—a fitting association for a shrine overlooking the Caspian. According to local tradition, Sultan-Epe was the son of Hakim-Ata, a renowned Sufi poet and disciple of Khoja Ahmed Yasawi, which connects the shrine to the spiritual tradition associated with Shopan-Ata and, through his later spiritual mentor, Beket-Ata.

The stacked-stone entrance to Sultan-Epe.

The final stop on the circuit is Karaman-Ata, where a seven-hectare necropolis stretches across a low, open plot of desert dotted with funerary monuments, some more than 1,000 years old. The mosque is carved into the slope of a small marl hill, and as you descend into the rock, the temperature drops noticeably from the heat outside. A narrow passage leads to a circular room believed to be associated with Sufi rituals including devotional whirling, a type of worship that is expressed through physical movement.

Karaman-Ata, the largest of the five rock mosques of Kazhakstan’s Mangystau desert.

Reaching each site still means long drives across remote terrain, with limited to no mobile coverage or fuel services–making a sturdy vehicle and a trusted local guide essential. But after days spent traveling the region, these places begin to alter your sense of the desert. Around a well, beneath a mulberry tree, or at a table crowded with food and strangers, the landscape that seemed so stark and inhospitable reveals an unexpected generosity, preserved in the rituals of those who continue to make the journey here.

Carson Daly Has a New ‘Today’ Gig—Inside the Sprawling Long Island Estate He Calls Home When He’s Not in the Studio

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Carson Daly’s weekends just got a whole lot busier—the “Today” host will join the Saturday edition of the show as a co-anchor.

The TV personality shared the news on the Nov. 5 episode of “Today,” noting that he’ll be joining existing co-anchor Laura Jarrett while upholding his weekday broadcast duties.

According to NBC News, Carson, 53, had been working as the interim “Saturday Today” anchor since September.

“I should just say, I’ve been very lucky to have a lot of TV gigs, but this one’s really special,” he said on the show. “At my age and at my stage of my career, to have an opportunity to come in and sit next to Laura and to learn and to get better at my job and be the best broadcaster that I can, it’s not something I take lightly.”

Nov. 7 will be Carson’s first official day as co-anchor.

“We’re going to have a lot of fun on Saturday,” he said. “But most importantly, the best part about the gig, like here during the week—which I’ll still be here during the week—is getting to go to your place of employment with such high-quality human beings. We are a family, and the fact that I get to spend one more day in this building, six days a week, is an absolute honor.”

When Carson isn’t spending time at the New York City studios that “Today” is filmed in, he has a beautiful family home in Manhasset, NY.

In 2013, the “Total Request Live” alum snapped up a six-bedroom, 5.5-bathroom estate for $6.5 million, according to The Sun. Carson purchased the home—which is about 25 miles northeast of Manhattan—for $6.5 million.

The Colonial-style abode is a gorgeous escape from the bustle of the city and includes a pool and a pool house.

Carson shared a glimpse inside the home in 2020, when he posted a snapshot of his family celebrating Easter. He noted that he was “especially grateful” for his family’s “blessings,” as he and his wife, Siri Daly, and their four young children virtually participated in their Catholic parish’s services next to a cozy fire in the living room.

“The Voice” host also shared some personal details about his sleeping arrangements with his wife, revealing they had a “sleep divorce” that was the “best thing that happened” to their marriage.

While his wife was expecting the couple’s fourth child in November 2019, Carson told “Today” viewers why the pair choose to seek out separate beds.

“I have sleep apnea, my wife’s pregnant, and during a home renovation, we downgraded to a queen-size bed. We just felt like we’re better off sleeping in different beds,” he said.

He joked that his CPAP machine makes a shared bed feel “like sleeping next to Darth Vader.”

“Most women would want to sleep in another bedroom,” he said. “I don’t snore anymore, but the noise of the machine keeps her up.”

In 2022, Carson confirmed that the arrangement continued after the baby was born.

“We both, admittedly, slept better apart,” he said on the “Today” show in 2022.

Siri is also part of the “Today” crew, where she works as a regular food contributor.

On social media, the chef and cookbook author often shares content from the family’s gorgeous kitchen, which has loads of cabinet space, sprawling counters, a subway tile backsplash, and even a “baking nook.”

In November 2023, Siri posted about her love of crafting intricate holiday tablescapes, joking that “no one is allowed to eat and mess it up,” while showing the tastefully decorated dining room.

TikTok launches standalone TikTok Shop app (in four markets)

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

TikTok has spent years making it easier to buy the products that appear in makeup tutorials, cooking videos, and creator recommendations in TikTok Shop. Now, it’s giving shoppers a separate app to open when they're in the mood to spend on a new lip gloss, a milk frother, or another pair of sneakers.

The company has (rather quietly) released a standalone TikTok Shop app in Singapore, Malaysia, Thailand, and the Philippines on iOS and Android, as social media consultant Matt Navarra first spotted on X. The launch gives TikTok's growing shopping business its own place on a phone's home screen.

According to its Apple App Store listing, the app offers personalized product recommendations, livestreams and short videos showing products in action, checkout, order tracking, and 24/7 customer support. Shoppers are also being offered app-exclusive deals, free shipping promotions, and first-order perks.

The Google Play listing shows more than 50,000 downloads, while the Singapore Apple App Store lists it at No. 71 in the shopping category, with a single five-star rating. Its latest iOS update, version 45.5.5, arrived just 13 hours before publication (at 1 p.m. ET on Nov. 5).

The app's Singapore App Store listing names TikTok Pte. Ltd. as its provider, a separate company from TikTok USDS Joint Venture, which operates TikTok in the U.S. The majority-American-owned joint venture handles U.S. data security, recommendation algorithms, and content moderation, while TikTok's global business still manages commercial activities, including e-commerce.

It's unclear whether the standalone shopping app will launch in the U.S. TikTok has not yet responded to Mashable's request for comment.

Giving Shop a dedicated app could help TikTok compete for the moments when someone opens Amazon or Temu intending to browse or buy. Videos and livestreams remain part of the experience, according to the listings, but shopping becomes the reason to open the app in the first place. The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile Products available for purchase through affiliate links. If you buy something through links on our site, Mashable may earn an affiliate commission.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

That fits into a broader expansion Mashable has been following, as TikTok builds more services around the recommendations people encounter on its platform.

In May, TikTok launched TikTok GO's hotel-booking features, which let U.S. adults book accommodations, tours, and attractions through partners including Booking.com, Expedia, and Viator. Creators can also earn commissions through eligible content, giving a travel recommendation a more direct connection to a reservation.

TikTok's announcement describes a process that takes people from discovering a place in a video to checking availability and booking it. As CEO of TikTok USDS Joint Venture Adam Presser said at the time, the platform is "good for creators, good for local businesses, and good for communities."

And more recently, TikTok launched Must Eat, TikTok's restaurant guide for the areas around New York City, Los Angeles, and the San Francisco Bay Area. Built from more than 40 million posts, reviews, and comments, it organizes food recommendations into searchable lists, with OpenTable reservations available for select restaurants. A video of a particularly good dinner can influence choosing and booking a future reservation.

TikTok has also been experimenting with giving specific kinds of entertainment their own apps. Its PineDrama service, launched earlier this year, puts short dramas into a separate app with personalized recommendations and tools for browsing series and episodes.

Taken together, those products suggest TikTok sees room to build entire services around habits that started in the feed. The standalone Shop app puts that idea to another test, asking whether people who discover products on TikTok will also choose it when shopping is what they came to do.

From YouTubers and TikTok stars to streamers and podcasters, Mashable talks to creators about how they built their platforms, the gear they swear by, and the trends they see coming next. 13 Easy Holiday Mocktail Recipes For Entertaining Guests (Or Just Yourself)

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Sometimes you just need a special bevvie.

I love a good drink — whether it’s a canned cocktail or a jam jargarita, sign me up. But even I sometimes fall back on a good old mocktail, whether I just can’t handle a red wine hangover tomorrow or because I’m hosting and there needs to be an NA option. These easy holiday mocktail recipes are perfect for those moments when you need to whip up a big batch of something festive to drink that’s safe for the whole family. Some are nonalcoholic versions of cocktails you know and love, while others are just fun, festive drinks to serve in place of something spiked.

Since they make nonalcoholic and boozy ginger beer, ginger beer-based recipes are some of the easiest to make into mocktails on the fly. All The Healthy Things’ bevvie is bubbly, fruity, and slightly herby — and it looks so stinking cute with “frosted” cranberries on top.

I love it when a mocktail recipe makes up for its lack of booze with an overabundance of fun in another form. In Averie Cooks’ recipe for caramel apple cider, it’s a homemade caramel sauce all around the rim — I’m in. You could totally use store-bought sauce to save time, but if you have to simmer the cider anyway, it might be fun to try.

If you’re making drinks for guests who don’t mind something super sweet (kids will lose it for this stuff), you’ve got to make Princess Pinky Girl’s Grinch punch. It’s a bright green blend of sherbet, green Hawaiian Punch, Sprite, and juice, so it will hurt many people’s teeth. But sweet-toothed friends, this one’s for you.

Holiday party snuck up on you and you’re in charge of drinks? Whip up this super easy holiday punch from Damn Delicious. All you have to do is mix together some ginger ale, pineapple juice, and cranberry juice, and load it up with some fresh fruits before serving.

Want a more decadent drink for a movie marathon at home? You have to try Princess Pinky Girl’s apple cider float recipe. It’s basically like a milkshake and apple cider had a baby and then dressed it up in warm, seasonal spices.

Think about how happy you’d be if you showed up at a holiday party and there was a self-serve vat of hot chocolate waiting for you. Well, be the change you wish to see in the world with Princess Pinky Girl’s Crockpot peppermint hot chocolate. While technically not a mocktail in that it’s not imitating a cocktail, a gussied-up take on hot chocolate still feels really festive and exciting to try.

This apple cider shrub mocktail from Budget Bytes is the drink if you like a not-so-sweet mocktail that tastes almost like it could be boozy. The drink has a few steps that’ll make you look like a professional bartender, but it’s easy enough that even a first-timer around a cocktail shaker can pull it off. Bonus points: They’re also incredibly cheap to make.

Dirty soda, as a drink category, got plenty of attention this year thanks to the cast of The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives. So if you need a fun NA drink to serve your reality TV-loving friends, what could be better? Princess Pinky Girl’s recipe will teach you how to get the perfect balance of flavors if it’s your first time wading into dirty soda territory.

Haute & Healthy Living’s pomegranate sparkler looks like the perfect thing to swirl around in one hand while you make a cozy dinner. It still feels special — when else do most of us spring for pomegranate juice? — and has seasonal touches that give you all the festive feels of a cocktail.

Our Love Language Is Food’s spiced cider mocktail proves that as long as you have a fancy glass and a sugar-and-spice rim, you can make your beverage feel celebratory. These just look aggressively clink-able.

Want to make a big batch of punch for the party? Haute & Healthy Living’s recipe blends apple juice, orange concentrate, and cranberry cocktail, plus a few extra touches to bring it to life. It’s so easy to make, you can refill the bowl in a few minutes mid-party.

If you just can’t with all the sweet this and spiced that, Simply LaKita’s blackberry thyme mocktail is perfect for you. It’s simple, fizzy, and way less dessert-in-a-glass-inspired that many winter mocktails tend to be.

Which mocktails are you adding to your hosting repertoire this year?
